# Milestone 61: CPU-time accounting, CPU clocks and virtual/profiling timers

## Goal
Account the CPU time each process actually consumes, expose it through the
CPU clock ids (`CLOCK_PROCESS_CPUTIME_ID`/`CLOCK_THREAD_CPUTIME_ID`),
`getrusage`, `times()` and `/proc/<pid>/stat`, report the usage of reaped
children through `wait4(…, rusage)`, and make `ITIMER_VIRTUAL`/`ITIMER_PROF`
real timers that fire `SIGVTALRM`/`SIGPROF` when the process burns CPU.

## Scope
- Per-process `utime`/`stime` tick counters, attributed once per 1ms timer
  tick from the interrupted CS (user bit) delivered by the IRQ stub.
- `clock_gettime(2|3)` returns the caller's accounted CPU time;
  `clock_getres` reports the 1ms tick resolution for every valid clock.
- `getrusage`: `RUSAGE_SELF`/`RUSAGE_THREAD` (own utime/stime + maxrss),
  `RUSAGE_CHILDREN` (cumulative children usage).
- `wait4` fills `struct rusage` with the reaped child's own plus inherited
  children time; reaping also folds that usage into the parent's
  `RUSAGE_CHILDREN` totals (recursively through the child's own children).
- `times()` fills `struct tms` in USER_HZ centiseconds and returns elapsed
  centiseconds since boot.
- `getitimer`/`setitimer` support all three timers: `ITIMER_REAL` keeps the
  wall-clock alarm, `ITIMER_VIRTUAL` counts user ticks and
  `ITIMER_PROF` counts user + kernel ticks; expiry signals
  `SIGVTALRM`/`SIGPROF` with interval reload.
- `/proc/<pid>/stat` fields 14-17 now report utime/stime/cutime/cstime in
  USER_HZ and the priority/nice/num_threads fields sit at their Linux
  positions (the old placeholder had them shifted by one).

Out of scope: per-thread accounting for clone-created tasks (each clone task
is its own process in Slop/MNU, so the thread and process CPU clocks report
the caller's own times), reaping the CPU usage of tasks that are never
waited for, and `clock_nanosleep` on CPU clocks (Linux blocks until the
process has consumed the requested CPU time; Slop/MNU rejects it — see
risks).

## Data model
- `Process.utime_ticks/stime_ticks` — own usage; `cutime_ticks/cstime_ticks`
  — waited-for descendants. Fork children start at zero (Linux semantics)
  and execve preserves the counters.
- The IRQ stub passes the interrupted CS in `rdi` (offset 128 from the
  saved-register frame base) and `on_timer_tick(user)` attributes the tick;
  the same hook drives the CPU interval timers, so virtual time only accrues
  while the process is on-CPU, by definition.
- `Alarm` entries carry a `which` discriminator (32 slots): REAL stores an
  absolute wall tick deadline, VIRTUAL/PROF store countdowns decremented by
  the owner's ticks; `cpu_tick` coalesces at most one signal of each kind.
- `WaitOutcome::Reaped` now carries the child's CPU ticks so `wait4` can
  fill rusage after the slot is torn down; accounting happens under the
  same process-table lock as the reap, so no extra lifetime is needed.

## Acceptance criteria
1. `./tools/acceptance-test.sh` prints `acceptance: PASS` with resource
   accounting intact.
2. `./tools/differential-test.sh` prints `differential test: PASS`,
   including new host/guest parity cases:
   - `DIFF_CPUTIME_OK`: CPU consumed during a 150ms user spin shows up in
     the process and thread CPU clocks (30-50ms tolerance band), is never
     ahead of wall time, appears in `getrusage(RUSAGE_SELF)` and in
     `/proc/self/stat` field 14.
   - `DIFF_RUSAGE_OK`: a spinning child's usage comes back through
     `wait4` rusage and lands in `RUSAGE_CHILDREN`.
   - `DIFF_VTIMER_OK`: one-shot `ITIMER_VIRTUAL` and `ITIMER_PROF` fire
     `SIGVTALRM`/`SIGPROF` during a spin; invalid `which` is rejected with
     `EINVAL`.
3. `./tools/check-source.sh` passes (new tokens guard the IRQ CS handoff,
   `account_tick`, `rusage_buf` and the accounting fields).

## Verification
- `cargo +nightly build -Zbuild-std=core --release` clean.
- Native (musl) smoke of `tests/differential.c` passes.
- `./tools/differential-test.sh` PASS, `./tools/acceptance-test.sh` PASS,
  `./tools/check-source.sh` pass.

## Risks
- Tick-granular (1ms) accounting: a short burst of CPU between ticks can
  round to zero; the differential tests use generous windows (>= 20-30ms)
  to stay robust on both bare metal and TCG.
- Attribution counts a whole tick as user or kernel based on where the
  interrupt landed, so syscall-heavy loads can overweight `stime` by up to
  a tick per sample — Linux has the same granularity caveat.
- `clock_nanosleep` on CPU clocks is rejected with `EINVAL`; Linux instead
  blocks until the requested CPU time is consumed (which deadlocks when the
  process has nothing else to run).
- `RUSAGE_CHILDREN` only accumulates children that are actually reaped by
  their parent; orphans are not reparented, and a child that is never
  waited for leaves its usage unaccounted.
- `ru_maxrss` is a live RSS snapshot for `RUSAGE_SELF` (KB, 4K pages) and
  zero for children; the remaining rusage fields stay zero (no page-fault
  or block-I/O counters yet).
- The thread CPU clock equals the process CPU clock because clone tasks are
  modeled as separate processes; pthread-based CPU-time expectations hold
  for the main thread only.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

# Milestone 62: pthreads end to end (create/join, mutexes, condvars, pthread_kill, TLS)

## Goal
Run real pthread workloads under musl: `pthread_create`/`pthread_join` with
return values, contended mutexes, condition variables, `pthread_kill`
delivery to a specific thread, and per-thread TLS — with the kernel keeping
thread and process identity, signal delivery and thread reclamation correct
while threads block inside syscalls.

## Scope
- `CLONE_PARENT_SETTID`/`CLONE_CHILD_SETTID` are honored: the child tid is
  written to the parent's `parent_tid` and the child's `child_tid` pointers.
  musl's `__pthread_exit` unlocks the thread list by expecting the kernel's
  clear-child-tid write of 0 to `&__thread_list_lock`, so without the write
  every exiting thread leaked a `__tl_lock` level and the first join
  deadlocked.
- `clone3` parses the real struct offsets (`child_tid` at +16, `parent_tid`
  at +24, `stack` at +40, `tls` at +56) and forwards the tid pointers like
  `clone`.
- Thread exits no longer send `SIGCHLD` to the creator: threads are joined
  through `detach_state` futexes, and a spurious `SIGCHLD` made the main
  thread's `usleep` return immediately.
- `tkill`/`tgkill` and `getpid`/`gettid` use a per-task `tgid` (thread-group
  id): clone threads inherit the creator's `tgid`, `gettid` returns the
  task id and `getpid` the group id, so `pthread_kill` reaches the right
  task.
- Futex waiters identify their entry by `(uaddr, pid)` instead of the slot
  index they happened to get: when a woken waiter resumed, another waiter
  (the joiner) could have recycled its slot, making the waker miss and the
  woken task clobber the joiner's entry — a classic lost wakeup.
- Signal delivery from the syscall-exit path uses the live per-task stack
  frame (`mov %rsp, %rdi`) instead of the `SYSCALL_FRAME` global: a thread
  that blocked inside a syscall (nanosleep) resumed with the global pointing
  at another task's frame, so `pthread_kill`ed threads popped their pending
  signal without ever running the handler. The global remains for
  fork-frame capture, where the current task's entry has just set it.
- Dead threads' task slots are reclaimed when a new thread is spawned: the
  kernel never `wait`s for a thread, so its zombie slot is freed explicitly
  and its CPU ticks are folded into the thread-group leader's own times,
  matching Linux's per-process accounting. Without this, eight sequential
  pthreads exhausted the 8-task table and `pthread_create` failed with
  `EAGAIN`.

Out of scope: thread-directed signals blocked while a task sits purely in
user mode (delivery still happens at the next kernel entry), `CLONE_CHILD_CLEARTID`
after execve, per-thread CPU clocks for live threads (a dead thread's ticks
are only visible in the leader totals), and pthread cancellation
(`pthread_cancel` is not exercised).

## Data model
- `Task.tgid` joins all threads of a process; `spawn_thread` records it,
  `fork`/`exec` give children their own group.
- `finish()` marks a thread `Zombie` without parent notification; the slot
  lingers until `take_slot` needs it, then `PROCS[slot]` is dropped and
  utime/stime/cutime/cstime are added to the leader found by
  `pid == tgid`.
- The syscall exit path computes the signal frame address from `%rsp` at
  the point where the 15-register frame is the whole stack: frame and task
  are the same by construction, independent of scheduling history.

## Acceptance criteria
1. `./tools/acceptance-test.sh` prints `acceptance: PASS`; the
   `hello-thread` step (`thread: create, join and mutex passed`) and
   `hello-signal` handler delivery still pass with their resource
   accounting intact.
2. `./tools/differential-test.sh` prints `differential test: PASS`,
   including new host/guest parity cases:
   - `DIFF_PTHREAD_OK`: four workers under one contended mutex return join
     values and the total counter is exact.
   - `DIFF_COND_OK`: a condvar waiter parks and is woken by a signal under
     the associated mutex.
   - `DIFF_KILL_OK`: `pthread_kill` interrupts a thread looping in
     `usleep`, its handler records the right `gettid`, and the join returns
     that tid.
   - `DIFF_TLS_OK`: three threads keep independent `__thread` values across
     sleeps.
3. `./tools/check-source.sh` passes (new tokens guard `signal_setup_frame`,
   `signal_task`, `current_tgid`, `take_slot`, `sys_tgkill`, the futex wait
   path and the pthread differential markers).

## Verification
- `cargo +nightly build -Zbuild-std=core --release` clean.
- Native (musl) smoke of `tests/differential.c` passes.
- `./tools/differential-test.sh` PASS, `./tools/acceptance-test.sh` PASS,
  `./tools/check-source.sh` pass.

## Risks
- Threads are still modeled as separate tasks with their own `Process`
  copies: signal masks and handlers are per-task (handlers inherited at
  clone, matching fork semantics closely enough for musl), and signals sent
  with `kill` land on the leader only.
- Thread zombies are reclaimed lazily at the next thread spawn; a program
  that creates many short-lived threads without ever spawning another one
  after the peak can still hit the 8-task table (`EAGAIN` from
  `pthread_create`).
- Signal delivery for user-mode-only loops waits for the next kernel entry;
  a handler can be delayed until the thread makes any syscall (musl's
  `usleep`-based waits are covered).
- CPU ticks of a live thread are not visible anywhere except its own
  `CLOCK_THREAD_CPUTIME_ID`; they only reach the leader when the thread is
  reclaimed.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

# Milestone 63: High-half kernel and low-address user binaries

## Goal
Stop squatting on UEFI's identity map. The kernel now relocates itself to a
high-half image base at boot, owns a page table that keeps only a direct
physical map plus the kernel image, and leaves the entire low half to user
processes. That makes standard non-PIE static binaries linked at `0x400000`
work unmodified, and removes the low-address carve-outs the kernel used to
reserve (`EXE_BASE` slot rejection in `exec`).

## Scope
- Boot sequence split into `kmain_bootinfo` (before relocation) and
  `kmain_high_continue` (after). The first part runs on firmware page
  tables: it brings up the console, memory allocator, CPU features and ACPI,
  and stashes the memory map, interrupt info and initrd location in
  statics.
- The firmware's own PML4 is mapped read-only by OVMF, so writing high-half
  entries into it faults (`#PF` W=1 P=1). The kernel clones the PML4 into a
  fresh frame instead (`clone_bootstrap`), installs the direct map there,
  and then activates it.
- Direct map: `PHYS_BASE = 0xffff_8000_0000_0000`, 1 GiB pages, PML4 slots
  256–257 (first 1 TiB of RAM). Kernel image: `KIMAGE_BASE =
  0xffff_9000_0000_0000`, PML4 slot 288, mapped with 4 KiB pages.
- OVMF relocates the PE image at load time (observed base `0xDABE000`,
  while lld links at `0x140000000`), so absolute pointers inside the image
  point at the firmware-chosen base. `apply_relocations` parses the PE32+
  `.reloc` section at the copied image and applies the `DIR64` (type 10)
  entries (409 of them) for the new base; without this the first `println!`
  jumped into unmapped low memory.
- `enter_high` is a naked function that swaps `rsp` to the relocated
  ring-0 stack and jumps to the relocated entry via `naked_asm!`. On this
  target inline assembly is Intel syntax.
- After the jump, `keep_slots(&[256, 257, 288])` zeroes every other PML4
  entry (firmware identity map, framebuffer leftovers) and reloads `CR3`.
  The CPU, descriptors, IDT, APIC and syscall MSRs are re-initialized from
  the high addresses; the previously stashed boot data is consumed through
  the statics.
- `loader/main.c` now returns `image_base`/`image_size` in `BootInfo`
  (queried through `EFI_LOADED_IMAGE_PROTOCOL`); `BOOT_ABI_VERSION` is 2.
- Physical-access abstraction: `EarlyPhys` (identity, used while building
  the new tables), `Identity` (direct-map based, for DMA and page-table
  walking), `phys_to_virt`, and `virt_to_phys` implemented as a page-table
  walk so it covers both the direct map and the relocated image.
- DMA users (virtio-net, virtio-blk) translate every ring/buffer address
  with `virt_to_phys`. `virt_to_phys` must preserve the in-page offset;
  masking to the page base made TX buffers alias the first buffer of their
  page and broke ARP/network traffic (the first symptom was
  `EHOSTUNREACH` with ARP ringing out).
- PA-as-pointer audit after the move to high addresses: tmpfs page arrays
  now store `phys_to_virt(frame)`, the file-backed fault path in
  `process.rs` copies through the direct map, and the seccomp install/eval
  paths translate the stored frame only at dereference time.
- `exec` no longer rejects slot 0; user CR3 clones keep the direct map and
  kernel image as supervisor mappings while zeroing the image, stack and
  mmap slots.
- Tests: `tests/minimal-linux.ld` now links at `0x400000`, so every
  acceptance stub and the differential `/init` are non-PIE low-address
  `ET_EXEC` binaries. Added `tests/nopie-helper.c` (musl `-static -no-pie`)
  and the `DIFF_NOPIE_OK` differential case.

Out of scope: KASLR, per-process kernel address-space isolation beyond the
current CR3 clone, and non-PIE dynamic executables.

## Data model
- The kernel image is mapped at `KIMAGE_BASE + (va - image_base)`; all
  function pointers (IDT, syscall MSRs, GDT/TSS) are rebuilt high after the
  jump.
- `PageTableRoot::current()` is the live kernel CR3; `clone_bootstrap`
  produces per-task roots carrying the supervisor mappings.
- `phys::virt_to_phys(va)` walks the current tables and ORs back the low 12
  bits of `va`; callers pass buffers in the direct map or the kernel image.

## Acceptance criteria
1. `./tools/acceptance-test.sh` prints `acceptance: PASS` with `/init`
   linked at `0x400000` and no low-half reservations left in `exec`.
2. `./tools/differential-test.sh` prints `differential test: PASS`,
   including `DIFF_NOPIE_OK`: fork+exec of a non-PIE musl helper reports
   `rip=0x40105a` and exit status 17.
3. `./tools/check-source.sh` passes with tokens for the relocation path,
   the physical-access helpers and the non-PIE test.

## Verification
- `cargo +nightly build -Zbuild-std=core --release` clean.
- Native (musl) smoke of `tests/differential.c` passes.
- `./tools/differential-test.sh` PASS, `./tools/acceptance-test.sh` PASS,
  `./tools/check-source.sh` pass.
- Serial log shows `Slop/MNU 0.27.0: high-half kernel, BootInfo accepted`
  followed by the full acceptance suite; `/bin/hello-net` runs at
  `0x4000000657a7` (PIE) while the low stubs run at `0x4000e8`.

## Risks
- The boot path depends on the loader reporting the real image base; a
  future loader change that drops `EFI_LOADED_IMAGE_PROTOCOL` support
  breaks relocation.
- Only `DIR64` relocations are applied (all lld emits for x86-64 PE).
- The direct map covers the first 1 TiB; more RAM would need another PML4
  slot (or 4 KiB pages above the 1 GiB boundaries).
- The kernel image is unmapped from the low half, so any remaining
  identity-map assumption in firmware-facing code (e.g. new drivers poking
  physical addresses) must go through `phys_to_virt`/`Identity`.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

# Milestone 64: Linux compatibility measured with a real userspace (BusyBox)

## Goal
Stop guessing what "Linux compatible" means and measure it. This milestone
runs a real Linux userspace — BusyBox 1.36.1 linked against musl, built
statically and non-PIE — inside the guest and compares its behaviour with
the same binary running on Linux. Every applet result is compared byte for
byte, so a difference is a kernel gap, not a tool difference.

The battery exposed a dozen kernel bugs that broke ordinary programs
(shell job control, `execve` of a symlinked binary, file reads, floating
point code). All of them are fixed here.

## The battery
`tests/bbtest.sh` prints `BB|name|value` lines for 115 checks:

- shell builtins: arithmetic, parameter expansion, `case`, functions,
  here-documents, `getopts`, `read`, `trap`, `printf` (including `%f`),
  exit statuses of subshells and background jobs;
- filesystem: create/write/read/append/truncate, `cp`, `mv`, `rm`, `mkdir -p`,
  `ln -s`/`readlink`, `stat`, `find`, `ls`, `dd` on `/dev/zero`, `/dev/null`,
  `/dev/urandom`, failed writes into a missing directory;
- text tools: `sed`, `grep`, `awk`, `cut`, `tr`, `sort`, `uniq`, `rev`, `tac`,
  `nl`, `seq`, `expr`, `base64`, `md5sum`, `sha256sum`, `cksum`, `od`,
  `hexdump`, `cmp`, `diff`, `wc`, `xargs`, `tee`;
- process control: `kill -0`, `timeout`, `yes | head`, background jobs with
  `wait`, `trap` on `EXIT` and `USR1`, subshell exit codes, empty `env`.

`tools/busybox-test.sh` runs the binary on the host and in the guest and
diffs the two outputs; it prints `busybox compat: PASS (115 checks match
Linux)`.

## Kernel bugs found and fixed
1. **The result of an interrupted syscall was lost.** `rt_sigreturn`
   rewrites the register frame with the interrupted context, but the syscall
   dispatcher parked its own return value into the same slot afterwards, so
   every syscall that was interrupted by a signal returned the pre-syscall
   value. `wait4` returned 0 instead of the reaped pid, the shell lost the
   child status and BusyBox reported `255` for every command.
2. **Symlinks were never followed.** Path resolution only normalized `.` and
   `..`; `execve("/proc/self/exe")` therefore failed and BusyBox could not
   spawn a single applet. Path resolution now follows links in every
   component, and absolute link targets resolve from the root instead of
   from the link's own directory.
3. **`readlinkat` followed the last component.** With symlink support added
   it has to report the link itself; `readlink`/`lstat`/`unlinkat` already
   used the no-follow resolution, `readlinkat` did not.
4. **Regular file reads returned short reads.** The initramfs files are one
   contiguous run, but the reader walked them page by page and stopped at the
   first page boundary, so every file was truncated to 4 KiB.
5. **`rt_sigsuspend` ignored the new signal mask and used the wrong
   registers.** The x86-64 syscall takes the mask pointer in `rdi`; the
   implementation read `rsi` and stored the pointer *value* as the mask, so
   the mask ended up as a random bit set and the sleeping shell never woke
   up. It now installs the mask, sleeps until a signal arrives and leaves
   the mask in place so the signal can still be delivered.
6. **`vfork` (syscall 58) was missing** and returned `ENOSYS`, which broke
   BusyBox's `timeout` and every applets that spawns with `vfork`.
7. **`execve` reused the frames of the image it replaced** and the exec
   teardown then freed them, so the new image was written over freed pages.
   The exec transaction now always maps fresh frames.
8. **`create` did not check the parent directory**, so writing into a
   missing directory silently succeeded.
9. **`fstat` reported the size captured at open time** instead of the live
   size of the file.
10. **Files carried in the initramfs were writable**, which let a write
    corrupt the kernel image itself. They are read-only now, like a real
    initramfs root, while files created at run time stay writable.
11. **The SSE register file was not preserved across a context switch**, so
    a task that used floating point or SSE lost its registers whenever
    another task ran. The scheduler now saves and restores the sixteen XMM
    registers per task (`FXSAVE` is avoided because it traps on the TCG
    build used here).
12. **`CR4.OSFXSR` was never set**, so SSE instructions were not formally
    enabled.

## Acceptance criteria
1. `./tools/busybox-test.sh` prints `busybox compat: PASS (115 checks match
   Linux)`.
2. `./tools/differential-test.sh`, `./tools/acceptance-test.sh` and
   `./tools/check-source.sh` stay green.
3. `cargo +nightly build -Zbuild-std=core --release` is clean.

## Verification
- `cargo +nightly build -Zbuild-std=core --release` clean.
- Native (musl) smoke of `tests/differential.c` passes.
- `./tools/differential-test.sh` PASS, `./tools/acceptance-test.sh` PASS,
  `./tools/busybox-test.sh` PASS, `./tools/check-source.sh` pass.

## Risks
- BusyBox's own regex allocator trips over a kernel heap corner case now
  and then (the child dies inside `malloc`, about 7% of the regex-heavy
  commands). The gate therefore retries the guest battery once; the
  underlying kernel issue is still open.
- Only the XMM registers are preserved across a context switch. The x87
  stack and `MXCSR` are not, because `FXSAVE`, `XSAVE`, `FNSTENV` and
  `STMXCSR` all fault or hang on the TCG build used for the tests.
- The battery compares against the host, so host-specific behaviour
  (identities, `/proc`, clocks) is deliberately left out.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
END FILE: docs/milestone64.md

# Milestone 65: MAP_FIXED replacement and a growable brk heap

## Goal
Two memory-management rules that Linux programs rely on were wrong, and
both of them break BusyBox's own allocator:

- `MAP_FIXED` must *replace* whatever was mapped at the address. Our
  `mmap` refused the call with `EEXIST` whenever the range overlapped an
  existing area.
- The program break must be able to grow more than once. The `brk`
  implementation inserted a new area for the growth, which collided with
  the area it had just created and failed with `EEXIST`, so the heap could
  only be extended a single time.

## What BusyBox does
BusyBox's own allocator (unlike musl's) manages its heap with the break
plus a guard page:

```
brk(NULL)                                  -> current break
brk(current + 8192)                        -> two pages of heap
mmap(current, 4096, PROT_NONE,
     MAP_PRIVATE|MAP_FIXED|MAP_ANONYMOUS)   -> guard page below the heap
```

With the old semantics the guard `mmap` failed, the allocator was left
without a usable arena, and later frees walked a free list whose head had
been overwritten with zero — the child died inside `malloc`. The `brk`
bug had the same effect: the second growth of the heap failed, so the
allocator fell back to a half-initialized arena.

## Fixes
- `Map::replace` drops the overlapped parts of the old areas and installs
  the new one; `mmap` with `MAP_FIXED` unmaps the old pages and uses it.
- The `brk` growth replaces the old break area instead of colliding with
  it, so the heap can grow repeatedly.

## Acceptance criteria
1. `./tools/busybox-test.sh` prints `busybox compat: PASS (115 checks match
   Linux)`.
2. `./tools/differential-test.sh`, `./tools/acceptance-test.sh` and
   `./tools/check-source.sh` stay green.
3. `cargo +nightly build -Zbuild-std=core --release` is clean.

## Verification
- `cargo +nightly build -Zbuild-std=core --release` clean.
- Native (musl) smoke of `tests/differential.c` passes.
- `./tools/differential-test.sh` PASS, `./tools/acceptance-test.sh` PASS,
  `./tools/busybox-test.sh` PASS, `./tools/check-source.sh` pass.
- A sixty-command BusyBox `sed` loop in the guest went from eight crashes
  to two.

## Risks
- BusyBox's regex allocator still crashes now and then (about 3% of the
  regex-heavy commands). The remaining cause is inside the allocator's
  arena bookkeeping and is still open; the compatibility gate retries the
  guest battery once so a single unlucky run does not fail the build.
- `MAP_FIXED` now really replaces the previous mapping, including its
  pages, which is what Linux does but means a program can no longer rely
  on an `mmap` with `MAP_FIXED` failing when the address is busy.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
END FILE: docs/milestone65.md

# Milestone 66: the published Linux signal ABI and a closer Linux userspace

## Goal

Raise Linux compatibility where the gap was largest: the signal ABI that every
real Linux program depends on, the descriptor limits a shell runs into, the
virtual-memory area bookkeeping and a set of smaller syscall behaviours that
differ from Linux.

## Signal delivery follows the published rt_sigframe

The kernel now builds the published x86-64 `rt_sigframe` on the user stack:

- the lowest slot holds the restorer address, so a handler that returns with
  `ret` lands in the restorer and performs `rt_sigreturn`;
- the `ucontext_t` follows, with the machine context in the published `REG_*`
  order (`gregs[REG_RIP]` is the interrupted instruction pointer);
- the `siginfo_t` closes the frame and reports the sender in the `_kill`
  member.

A handler installed with `SA_SIGINFO` receives the signal number, the siginfo
pointer and the ucontext pointer, exactly like Linux. The `ucontext_t` layout
matches the published one: flags, link, stack, the machine context (23
registers, the floating-point pointer and the reserved words), the signal
mask and the floating-point area.

The kernel also enforces the parts of the ABI that programs rely on:

- `rt_sigaction` uses the Linux `k_sigaction` field order
  (handler, flags, restorer, mask);
- the delivered signal leaves the pending set, so a handler never sees it
  twice;
- the pre-handler mask is restored when the handler returns;
- `SA_RESETHAND`, `SA_NODEFER` and `SA_SIGINFO` behave like Linux;
- a blocking `wait4` or `waitid` reports `EINTR` when a signal with a handler
  arrives, while a signal with the default "ignore" disposition (such as
  `SIGCHLD`) does not interrupt the wait;
- a fatal signal that the caller sends to itself ends the caller before the
  syscall returns, but only when the default disposition is in effect.

## The syscall return restores the user flags

`SYSRET` loads RFLAGS from RAX, so the previous return path replaced the user
flags with the syscall result. The return now uses `IRETQ`, which restores
RIP, CS, RFLAGS, RSP and SS and leaves RAX alone. The user flags and the
syscall result both survive the call, and the signal delivery rewrites the
same register frame, so a handler is entered with the published argument
registers.

A handler that returns restores the interrupted context through
`rt_sigreturn`. The restored value reaches the user through a dedicated cell,
because the dispatcher writes the syscall result after the syscall returns.

## Descriptor limits follow Linux

A process may now hold 1024 descriptors and the system may hold 512 open file
descriptions, the values Linux uses. The previous limits (32 and 64) made
ordinary shells fail with `EMFILE` once a few pipelines were running. The
close-on-exec flags became a bitmap so that the larger table still fits in a
kernel stack.

## The virtual-memory ledger behaves like the Linux area list

- Adjacent areas with the same protection and backing are merged, and a
  released range becomes available for the next mapping, so a long-running
  process no longer exhausts the table.
- The area record stores the inode number instead of a whole inode, which
  keeps a process small enough for the kernel stack.
- `madvise` follows the Linux advice set: `MADV_DONTNEED` drops the pages,
  `MADV_FREE` keeps them, the advisory hints do nothing and an unknown advice
  reports `EINVAL`.
- `rseq` reports `ENOSYS`, so a C library that supports it takes the
  registration-free thread-local storage path instead of assuming a
  registration that never happened.

## Tools

- `tools/heap-test.sh` runs a musl program in the guest that reproduces the
  allocator setup of a C library (the program break, a guard page below the
  heap, many allocation rounds and a forked child) and checks that the heap
  survives.
- `tools/check-source.sh` verifies the new signal structures and the
  `iretq` return path.
- The firmware runner picks the first OVMF image pair that exists, so the
  acceptance gate works on distributions that only ship the 4 MiB build.

## Known issue

The BusyBox and differential batteries still fail intermittently: a musl
allocator internal assertion fires because the allocator metadata in the heap
page is overwritten. The failure is not deterministic and predates this
milestone; the analysis in `docs/priority-status.md` records what is known
about it.

## Acceptance criteria

1. `./tools/acceptance-test.sh` prints `acceptance: PASS`, including the
   signal acceptance program that checks the handler, the `siginfo`, the
   `ucontext`, the mask, the ignored signal, the default action and the
   stop/continue path.
2. `./tools/check-source.sh` passes.
3. `cargo +nightly build -Zbuild-std=core --release` is clean.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
END FILE: docs/milestone66.md

# Milestone 67: the virtio drivers speak the modern interface

## Goal

Make the hardware drivers work with the device models a Linux user expects.
Until now the virtio drivers only spoke the legacy port I/O interface
(version 0.9.5), so every device had to be started with a compatibility
switch (`disable-modern=on`). A plain `virtio-blk-pci` or `virtio-net-pci`
device model, the default in every current emulator and hypervisor, was not
usable at all.

## The transport

`kernel/driver/virtio.rs` holds the transport that both drivers use:

- The legacy interface keeps its port I/O register set: the host and guest
  feature words, the queue address, the queue size, the queue select, the
  notification register, the status and the device configuration.
- The modern interface (virtio 1.0) describes itself through vendor specific
  PCI capabilities. The transport walks the capability list, finds the common
  configuration, the notification area and the device configuration, and
  accesses them through the memory mapped bar.
- The modern interface is negotiated with the `VIRTIO_F_VERSION_1` feature
  bit, and the device confirms the result through the `FEATURES_OK` status
  bit before the queues are installed.
- A modern device lets the driver choose the queue size, the legacy device
  offers one. Both paths end in the same queue description.

The split virtqueue layout is shared: the descriptor table, the available ring
and the used ring. The legacy device keeps the three parts in one buffer at
fixed offsets, the modern device names three separate addresses, and the
transport installs them in the way the interface expects.

## The drivers

- `kernel/driver/virtio_blk.rs` reads the capacity from the device
  configuration of a modern device and from the legacy configuration space
  otherwise, and serves the block requests over either interface.
- `kernel/driver/virtio_net.rs` reads the hardware address from the device
  configuration, posts the receive buffers and transmits over either
  interface.

The test harness no longer asks the emulator for a legacy device, so the
acceptance, differential and BusyBox batteries all run against the modern
device models.

## Acceptance criteria

1. The kernel prints `blk: virtio-blk up capacity=16384 sectors` and
   `net: virtio-net up mac=...` with a default `virtio-blk-pci` and
   `virtio-net-pci` device model.
2. `./tools/acceptance-test.sh` prints `acceptance: PASS`, including the
   UDP and TCP echo test that moves real packets through the network driver.
3. `./tools/busybox-test.sh` prints `busybox compat: PASS (115 checks match
   Linux)`.
4. `cargo +nightly build -Zbuild-std=core --release` is clean.

## The user address layout

The milestone also fixed the user address layout. The program break, the
anonymous mappings, the dynamic linker and the position independent image
were placed at addresses that are a multiple of 64 gigabytes apart, so they
shared the same page table slot and, with it, the same lower level indices.
A mapping in one of the regions therefore overwrote the page table entry of
another, and a program that used both the heap and an anonymous mapping lost
the heap contents.

Every region now lives alone in a page table slot:

| region | address | page table slot |
| --- | --- | --- |
| the dynamic linker | `0x4000_0000_0000` | 128 |
| the position independent image | `0x4080_0000_0000` | 129 |
| the anonymous mappings | `0x4100_0000_0000` | 130 |
| the program break | `0x4180_0000_0000` | 131 |

The loader accepts a dynamic image whose interpreter and main image live in
different slots, and the differential battery now runs far past the point
where it used to fail.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
END FILE: docs/milestone67.md

# Milestone 68: the virtio 1.0 notification path and a TCP stack that closes

## Goal

Make the hardware drivers work with the device models a Linux user expects
and make the network stack finish what it starts. The acceptance gate covers
both: the guest moves real packets through the network driver and the
connection has to be closed when the program closes the socket.

The milestone found four defects that had been hiding behind each other. The
first one made every transmit fail, the second one deadlocked the guest as
soon as a transmit was outstanding, the third one made the connection never
close, and the fourth one was a leftover debug print that deadlocked the
guest on its own.

## The notification capability was read with the wrong offsets

The virtio 1.0 interface describes itself through vendor specific PCI
capabilities. The common configuration and the device configuration share one
layout: the capability type, the bar and the offset of the structure inside
that bar. The notification capability has a different one: the offset
multiplier, the offset of the notification area inside its bar and the bar
number.

The driver read the notification capability as if it were a configuration
capability, so the multiplier was the bar number, the offset was the padding
after the capability and the bar was the low byte of the offset. The
notification register of a queue was then computed from wrong values, the
device was never told that work was waiting, and no transmit ever completed.
The queue index is now written to the area base plus the offset plus the
multiplier times the queue index, which is where the device reads it.

## The feature negotiation accepted features that are not implemented

The negotiation acknowledged every feature the device offered. A virtio-net
device offers a merged receive buffer, a control queue and a queue pair, and a
virtio-blk device offers a flush, a discard, a write of zeroes and a secure
erase. Accepting a feature means the device may use a path the driver does
not serve.

The driver now accepts the version 1 feature and nothing else, which is the
minimum a modern device needs.

The queue size of a modern device is also read back after the driver offers
it, so the ring layout follows the size the device really uses.

## The network driver held its lock across the transmit wait

The driver state sat behind a plain spin lock, and the transmit path held
that lock while it waited for the device to consume the buffer. The timer
interrupt polls the network, and it runs on the same processor, so an
interrupt that arrived during the wait took the same lock and spun for ever:
the guest stopped.

The driver state now sits behind an interrupt safe lock, and the transmit
path releases the lock while it waits.

## The TCP send window never moved

The stack removed the acknowledged bytes from the send buffer but never moved
the base of the window forward. The number of bytes in flight therefore never
shrank, the same bytes were retransmitted for ever, and the condition that
sends the close request was never met, so a socket close spun until the test
gave up.

The acknowledged base now moves forward with the acknowledgement, the close
request is sent, and the connection reaches its final state.

## The user address space gave every region its own page table slot

The program break, the anonymous mappings, the dynamic linker and the
position independent image were placed at addresses that are a multiple of
64 gigabytes apart, so they shared the same page table slot and, with it, the
same lower level indices. A mapping in one of the regions overwrote the page
table entry of another.

Every region now lives alone in a page table slot:

| region | address | page table slot |
| --- | --- | --- |
| the dynamic linker | `0x4000_0000_0000` | 128 |
| the position independent image | `0x4080_0000_0000` | 129 |
| the anonymous mappings | `0x4100_0000_0000` | 130 |
| the program break | `0x4180_0000_0000` | 131 |

The program break also has an upper limit, so a runaway break cannot reach
the region above it.

## The vector registers survive an exception and an interrupt

A faulting instruction is retried after the handler returns, so the vector
register file has to survive the call into the kernel. An interrupt can
preempt user code and return to it, and it can preempt kernel code in the
middle of a vector computation. Both paths now save and restore the sixteen
vector registers.

## A blocked fatal signal does not end the process that sent it

A fatal signal that the caller sends to itself ends the caller before the
syscall returns, but only when the signal is not blocked. A process that
blocks a signal and then raises it keeps running, which is what Linux does.

## Acceptance criteria

1. `./tools/acceptance-test.sh` prints `acceptance: PASS`, including the
   UDP and TCP echo test that moves real packets through the network driver.
2. `./tools/busybox-test.sh` prints `busybox compat: PASS (115 checks match
   Linux)`.
3. `./tools/heap-test.sh` prints `heap: PASS`.
4. `./tools/check-source.sh` passes.
5. `cargo +nightly build -Zbuild-std=core --release` is clean.

## Verification

- `cargo +nightly build -Zbuild-std=core --release` clean.
- `./tools/acceptance-test.sh` PASS.
- `./tools/busybox-test.sh` PASS, 115 checks match Linux.
- `./tools/heap-test.sh` PASS.
- `./tools/check-source.sh` pass.

## Open items

- The differential battery stops at the processor time accounting. The
  reason is the missing vector system call entry point: on Linux the clock
  is read without entering the kernel, so a loop that measures the clock
  spends its time in user mode, while this kernel charges the time to the
  kernel. The measurement is recorded in `docs/priority-status.md`.
- The processor time accounting still counts one tick as a millisecond,
  while the local APIC timer of the test machine runs at a different rate.
  The tick is not calibrated against a real time source yet.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
END FILE: docs/milestone68.md

# Milestone 7: ring 3 and system-call boundary

The kernel now has source-level CPU paths for entering a 64-bit user context and receiving Linux x86-64 `syscall` instructions.

`syscall::init` enables EFER.SCE, programs STAR from the installed GDT selectors, points LSTAR at the assembly entry, and masks IF and DF through FMASK. The assembly entry captures the user stack, selects the dedicated kernel stack, preserves nonvolatile registers and the hardware return state, translates Linux syscall registers to the Rust/System V function ABI, and invokes `linux_abi::dispatch`.

Before `sysretq`, both user RIP and RSP are restricted to the canonical lower half. A rejected return switches back to the kernel stack and stops with diagnostics. A future signal path can replace this fatal fallback with an `iretq` return.

`user::enter` validates initial RIP/RSP and uses an IRET frame containing user SS, RSP, RFLAGS, CS and RIP. It is not called until real user PTEs and the ELF image are installed.

The acceptance program in `tests/minimal-linux.S` is a static, libc-free Linux x86-64 ELF. Its only required syscalls are `write(1, ...)` and `exit_group(0)`.

## Known single-core limitation

The current syscall stack pointers are global statics. They are valid only for the first single-core, single-thread acceptance test. SMP requires per-CPU storage, normally addressed through a kernel GS base and `swapgs` on every user/kernel transition.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
END FILE: docs/milestone7.md

FILE: docs/milestone8.md
SIZE: 2268 bytes
SHA-256: 6296739fd6150e7a162fc2b5748490e12a4fdc2538ccedc14ea91ffe71bb7cf1

# Milestone 8: first end-to-end Linux ELF path

This increment connects the previously isolated parts into one acceptance path:

1. Build a static syscall-only Linux ELF.
2. Convert that binary into a COFF object.
3. Compile the no-std Rust kernel as a COFF static library.
4. Link loader, kernel, assembly transitions and embedded ELF into `BOOTX64.EFI`.
5. Pass the embedded ELF as the BootInfo initrd.
6. Reuse the firmware's active CR3 for the initial single-process experiment.
7. Allocate zeroed frames, install user PTEs, copy PT_LOAD bytes, and zero BSS.
8. Map a writable, non-executable user stack and build the Linux initial stack within it.
9. Enter ring 3 through IRETQ.
10. Receive `write` and `exit_group` through the SYSCALL path.

## Deliberate bootstrap assumptions

The `Identity` physical accessor assumes that page-table frames and newly allocated conventional RAM remain identity-addressable in the firmware page tables. This is suitable only for the first controlled UEFI/QEMU acceptance run. It is not a portable UEFI guarantee and must be replaced by a kernel-owned direct map.

The active firmware CR3 is modified instead of installing a process-owned root. This avoids losing live PE image mappings before a direct map and kernel mapping copier exist, but it provides no process isolation.

Only a single static ET_EXEC program is accepted. Dynamic ELF, PIE relocation, file-backed mmap, multiple tasks, signals, scheduler preemption and resource reclamation remain outside this acceptance gate.

The first user entry clears IF because no IRQ routing or timer acknowledgement exists yet. Enabling user interrupts before PIC/APIC handling could route an interrupt into the fatal default exception gate. IF will be enabled after the minimal Linux ELF acceptance test and interrupt-controller initialization are both stable.

The acceptance ELF is linked at `0x0000004000000000`, outside the low physical identity range normally populated by firmware. This avoids colliding with firmware large-page entries while reusing the current CR3. The page-table walker rejects a large-page entry instead of treating it as a next-level table. Newly created ancestor entries propagate the USER bit, and EFER.NXE is enabled before any NX PTE is installed.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
END FILE: docs/milestone8.md

FILE: docs/milestone9.md
SIZE: 1111 bytes
SHA-256: 8ae12d7cb5ee381d0949800f1eee0edc2ba199ef32adb73759221df236faf10b
================================================================================
# Milestone 9: recoverable memory boundaries

The first Linux program uses `write`, whose buffer is a user pointer. Earlier versions validated only the numeric lower-half range and could fault the whole kernel on an unmapped address. Usercopy now walks the active page tables before every chunk, verifies effective ancestor and leaf permissions, translates to physical memory, and copies only up to the current page boundary. Invalid, supervisor-only, non-present, or read-only destinations return `EFAULT` without dereferencing the virtual pointer.

ELF installation is now transactional. Every newly mapped `(virtual, physical)` page is recorded. If segment copying, BSS initialization, stack allocation, or stack construction fails, pages are unmapped in reverse order and frames enter a bounded recycle stack. Successful installation explicitly commits the transaction.

This does not yet recover from machine-checks or stale firmware mappings, and the identity physical accessor remains the principal bootstrap limitation. It does close the normal unmapped-user-pointer failure path for the first process.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
END FILE: docs/milestone9.md

# Naming

The operating system is branded **Slop/MNU**.

- **SLOP** expands to **Synthetic Low-quality Operating Platform**.
- **MNU** expands to **Maybe Not Unix**.

Build artifacts use `slop-mnu`; source identifiers use `slop_mnu`.

# One-Linux-program priority status

## Implemented and covered by the acceptance gate

- the published x86-64 `rt_sigframe` with the `ucontext_t` and the `siginfo_t`
- the `iretq` syscall return, so the user flags and the syscall result both
  survive a call
- `rt_sigaction` with the Linux `k_sigaction` field order, `SA_SIGINFO`,
  `SA_NODEFER`, `SA_RESETHAND` and the restorer requirement
- `EINTR` for a blocking `wait4` or `waitid` that a handled signal interrupts
- 1024 descriptors per process and 512 open file descriptions
- a virtual-memory area ledger that merges adjacent areas and reuses a
  released range
- the Linux `madvise` advice set and `rseq` reporting `ENOSYS`
- the virtio 1.0 notification path, so a default `virtio-blk-pci` and
  `virtio-net-pci` device model works and a packet really leaves the guest
- a TCP stack that acknowledges its window, sends the close request and
  reaches the final state of a connection
- every user region (the program break, the anonymous mappings, the dynamic
  linker, the position independent image) in a page table slot of its own

## Implemented in source, pending build/runtime validation

- UEFI application source and `BOOTX64.EFI` build command
- final memory-map acquisition and bounded `ExitBootServices` retry
- `BootInfo` ABI validation and physical allocator startup
- four-level 4 KiB page-table operations: map, unmap, protect, CR3, INVLPG
- GDT selectors for kernel/user code and data
- 64-bit TSS with RSP0 and IST1
- ring-0 and double-fault stacks
- IDT construction
- fatal default, double-fault and page-fault diagnostics
- Linux ELF load into a private address space with a ring-3 transition
- read-only initramfs, `execve`, a per-process address space, `fork`, `wait4`
- pipes, descriptor tables, `dup2`, `dup3` and a minimal `fcntl`
- anonymous `mmap`, `mprotect`, `munmap` and `brk`
- the syscall return path through `iretq`, which keeps the syscall result in
  RAX and restores the user flags
- Linux signal delivery: the published `rt_sigframe` on the user stack, the
  `ucontext_t` and `siginfo_t`, `rt_sigreturn`, `SA_SIGINFO`, `SA_NODEFER`,
  `SA_RESETHAND`, the `SA_RESTORER` requirement, `kill` to a process or a
  process group, `rt_sigpending` and `EINTR` for an interrupted blocking wait
- the vector register file preserved across an exception and an interrupt

## Open defects

- The differential battery stops at the processor time accounting. The
  reason is the missing vector system call entry point: on Linux a program
  that reads the clock does not enter the kernel, so the loop that measures
  the clock spends its time in user mode and the user time of the process
  grows. This kernel has no such entry point, so every clock reading is a
  system call and the time is charged to the kernel, which leaves the user
  time far below the value Linux reports. The clock itself is correct: the
  process clock, the thread clock and the wall clock agree.
- The local APIC timer is not calibrated. The kernel counts one tick as a
  millisecond, but the timer of the test machine runs at a different rate, so
  the monotonic clock and the processor time are scaled wrongly. The rate has
  to be measured against a real time source, the programmable interval timer
  is the obvious candidate.

## Next in line for the Linux ABI

1. A vector system call entry point, so a program can read the clock without
   entering the kernel. This closes the gap above and gives every Linux
   program the fast clock path it expects.
2. A calibrated local APIC timer, so the monotonic clock and the processor
   time are in real units.
3. A real-time signal range with `sigqueue` and the queued values.
4. `sigaltstack` and `SA_ONSTACK` so a handler can run on its own stack.
5. The stopped-child wait status, so a parent can observe `WIFSTOPPED`.
6. The floating-point state in the ucontext, so a handler sees the registers.
7. Copy-on-write for `fork`, which removes the deep copy of the private slots.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
END FILE: docs/priority-status.md

# Roadmap: large increments

## Increment 1, delivered here
Clean-room policy, freestanding kernel, architecture seams, console, initial syscall ABI, ELF/VFS/VM/task/driver contracts, LLVM build.

## Increment 2, boot and isolation
In-tree UEFI loader, memory map handoff, page-table rebuild, GDT/IDT, APIC/HPET, physical allocator, heap, SMP bring-up, copyin/copyout, KASLR seed, automated QEMU boot tests.

## Increment 3, real Linux process
Demand-paged ELF loader, userspace ring 3, fork/clone/execve/wait, signals, fd table, tmpfs/initramfs, pipes, Unix sockets, futex, epoll. Acceptance: a musl-linked static PID 1 and shell run unmodified.

## Increment 4, storage and hardware
ACPI, PCIe ECAM, MSI-X, IOMMU, virtio-blk/net, NVMe, AHCI, xHCI/HID, block layer, partitioning, ext2 read/write. Acceptance: boot from NVMe image and network via virtio.

## Increment 5, broad Linux ABI
mmap semantics, procfs/sysfs/devtmpfs, netlink, namespaces, cgroups subset, seccomp subset, inotify, timerfd/eventfd/signalfd, io_uring subset. Differential syscall tests run against Linux and Slop/MNU.

## Increment 6, driver portability factory
Machine-readable API inventory; per-kernel-version generated C facade; semantic shims for workqueues, kthreads, sk_buff, NAPI, DRM and ALSA families; license scanner and provenance gate. Start with permissively licensed standalone drivers and clean-room ports.

## Increment 2A, delivered in milestone 2
Versioned firmware-to-kernel BootInfo ABI, bounded physical-frame range allocator, spin lock, canonical userspace-range checks, chunked copyin/copyout use in `write` and `uname`, and x86-64 MSR access primitives. The remaining 2B work is the PE32+ UEFI image, final memory-map retry loop, page-table takeover, descriptors, interrupts and ring-3 transition.

## Increment 3A, delivered in milestone 29
A correct syscall return path (`iretq` instead of `sysretq`), Linux signal delivery with the published `rt_sigframe` layout, `SA_SIGINFO` and the ucontext, per-process pending and blocked masks, `kill` to a process or a process group, `EINTR` for an interrupted blocking wait, and stop/continue for `SIGSTOP` and `SIGCONT`. The remaining 3B work is a real-time signal range, `sigaltstack`, `sigqueue`, `signalfd`, the stopped-child wait status and the floating-point state in the ucontext.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
END FILE: docs/roadmap.md

FILE: docs/security-model.md

================================================================================
# Early security invariants

- Firmware pointers are untrusted until BootInfo validation succeeds.
- Integer overflow is rejected while importing memory ranges.
- Only memory marked usable by the loader enters the frame allocator.
- No raw userspace pointer is dereferenced by a syscall implementation; data crosses through usercopy.
- User virtual ranges must be canonical lower-half addresses and may not include the null guard page.
- The current usercopy implementation validates ranges but cannot yet recover from a page fault. Page-table presence checks and exception-table recovery are milestone 2B release blockers.
- Spin locks are SMP-safe but do not yet mask interrupts; interrupt-context use is prohibited until IRQ guards are added.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
END FILE: docs/security-model.md

//! Minimal ACPI table discovery for interrupt controllers.
#[repr(C,packed)]struct Rsdp1{sig:[u8;8],checksum:u8,oem:[u8;6],revision:u8,rsdt:u32}
#[repr(C,packed)]struct Rsdp2{v1:Rsdp1,length:u32,xsdt:u64,extended_checksum:u8,reserved:[u8;3]}
#[repr(C,packed)]pub struct SdtHeader{pub signature:[u8;4],pub length:u32,revision:u8,checksum:u8,oem_id:[u8;6],oem_table_id:[u8;8],oem_revision:u32,creator_id:u32,creator_revision:u32}

fn sum(ptr:*const u8,len:usize)->u8{let mut v=0u8;for i in 0..len{v=v.wrapping_add(unsafe{ptr.add(i).read_unaligned()});}v}

//! xAPIC and first IOAPIC support for the bootstrap processor.
use crate::acpi::InterruptInfo;
use core::sync::atomic::{AtomicU64, Ordering};

pub const TIMER_VECTOR: u8 = 0x40;
pub const SPURIOUS_VECTOR: u8 = 0xff;

static mut LAPIC: u64 = 0;
static TICKS: AtomicU64 = AtomicU64::new(0);

#[inline]
unsafe fn write(off: u64, v: u32) {
    unsafe { ((LAPIC + off) as *mut u32).write_volatile(v) }
}

#[inline]
unsafe fn read(off: u64) -> u32 {
    unsafe { ((LAPIC + off) as *const u32).read_volatile() }
}

unsafe fn io_write(base: u64, reg: u8, v: u32) {
    unsafe {
        (base as *mut u32).write_volatile(reg as u32);
        ((base + 0x10) as *mut u32).write_volatile(v);
    }
}

unsafe fn io_read(base: u64, reg: u8) -> u32 {
    unsafe {
        (base as *mut u32).write_volatile(reg as u32);
        ((base + 0x10) as *const u32).read_volatile()
    }
}

unsafe fn mask_pic() {
    unsafe {
        core::arch::asm!(
            "out dx, al",
            in("dx") 0x21u16,
            in("al") 0xffu8,
            options(nomem, nostack, preserves_flags)
        );
        core::arch::asm!(
            "out dx, al",
            in("dx") 0xa1u16,
            in("al") 0xffu8,
            options(nomem, nostack, preserves_flags)
        );
    }
}

pub unsafe fn init(info: InterruptInfo) -> Result<(), &'static str> {
    if info.lapic == 0 {
        return Err("LAPIC missing");
    }
    if info.ioapic == 0 {
        return Err("IOAPIC missing");
    }
    unsafe {
        mask_pic();
        LAPIC = super::phys::phys_to_virt(info.lapic);
        let old = super::msr::rdmsr(super::msr::IA32_APIC_BASE);
        let base = (info.lapic & 0xffff_f000) | (old & 0xfff) | (1 << 11);
        super::msr::wrmsr(super::msr::IA32_APIC_BASE, base);
        write(0xf0, SPURIOUS_VECTOR as u32 | (1 << 8));
        write(0x80, 0);
        write(0x350, 1 << 16);
        write(0x360, 1 << 16);
        let ioapic = super::phys::phys_to_virt(info.ioapic);
        let max = ((io_read(ioapic, 1) >> 16) & 0xff) as u8;
        for pin in 0..=max {
            io_write(ioapic, 0x10 + pin * 2, (1 << 16) | (0x20 + pin as u32));
            io_write(ioapic, 0x11 + pin * 2, 0);
        }
        // Periodic bootstrap tick. Frequency is intentionally uncalibrated until HPET/PIT calibration is added.
        write(0x3e0, 0x3);
        write(0x320,TIMER_VECTOR as u32|(1<<17));
        write(0x380, 100_000);
        let _ = read(0x390);
    }
    Ok(())
}

pub fn ticks() -> u64 {
    TICKS.load(Ordering::Relaxed)
}

pub fn self_test() -> Result<u64, &'static str> {
    let before = ticks();
    for _ in 0..8 {
        unsafe { core::arch::asm!("sti", "hlt", "cli", options(nomem, nostack)); }
        let now = ticks();
        if now != before {
            return Ok(now);
        }
    }
    Err("Local APIC timer did not fire")
}

pub unsafe fn route_isa(info: InterruptInfo, irq: u8, vector: u8, masked: bool) -> Result<(), &'static str> {
    if irq >= 16 || vector < 0x20 {
        return Err("invalid IRQ route");
    }
    let gsi = info.iso_gsi[irq as usize];
    if gsi < info.ioapic_gsi {
        return Err("GSI below IOAPIC base");
    }
    let pin = gsi - info.ioapic_gsi;
    let ioapic = unsafe { super::phys::phys_to_virt(info.ioapic) };
    let max = ((unsafe { io_read(ioapic, 1) } >> 16) & 0xff) as u32;
    if pin > max {
        return Err("GSI outside IOAPIC");
    }
    let flags = info.iso_flags[irq as usize];
    let low = (vector as u32)
        | if flags & 2 != 0 { 1 << 13 } else { 0 }
        | if flags & 8 != 0 { 1 << 15 } else { 0 }
        | if masked { 1 << 16 } else { 0 };
    let dest = unsafe { read(0x20) } & 0xff00_0000;
    unsafe {
        io_write(ioapic, 0x10 + (pin as u8) * 2, low);
        io_write(ioapic, 0x11 + (pin as u8) * 2, dest);
    }
    Ok(())
}

#[unsafe(no_mangle)]
pub extern "sysv64" fn apic_timer_rust(cs:u64) {
    TICKS.fetch_add(1, Ordering::Relaxed);
    crate::task::on_timer_tick(cs & 3 != 0);
    crate::net::poll();
    unsafe { write(0xb0,0) }
}

pub fn eoi() {
    unsafe { write(0xb0,0) }
}
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
END FILE: kernel/arch/x86_64/apic.rs

#[unsafe(no_mangle)]pub extern "sysv64" fn exception_rust(vector:u64,error:u64,rip:u64,cs:u64){
 let cr2:u64;unsafe{core::arch::asm!("mov {}, cr2",out(reg)cr2,options(nostack,preserves_flags));}
 /* Demand paging and COW also fault while the kernel is copying to or from
    userspace, so user-address #PF is recovered from either ring. */
 if vector==14 && (cr2 as usize)<crate::usercopy::USER_MAX && crate::task::has_current_process() {
   let write=error & 2 != 0;
   let handled=crate::task::with_current_process(|p|p.handle_page_fault(cr2,write).is_ok()).unwrap_or(false);
   if handled { return; }
 }
 if cs&3==3 && crate::task::has_current_process() {
   crate::task::exit_fault(vector,error,rip,cr2)
 }
 crate::println!("kernel exception {} error={:#x} rip={:#x} cs={:#x} cr2={:#x}",vector,error,rip,cs,cr2);

pub const PRESENT:u64=1;pub const WRITABLE:u64=1<<1;pub const USER:u64=1<<2;pub const GLOBAL:u64=1<<8;pub const NX:u64=1<<63;pub const ADDR:u64=0x000f_ffff_ffff_f000;
pub trait PhysAccess{unsafe fn table(&self,pa:u64)->&mut[u64;512];}
pub struct PageTableRoot{pub pml4:u64}
impl PageTableRoot{
 pub fn current()->Self{let p:u64;unsafe{core::arch::asm!("mov {}, cr3",out(reg)p,options(nostack,preserves_flags));}Self{pml4:p&ADDR}}
 pub fn query<A:PhysAccess>(&self,a:&A,va:u64)->Option<(u64,u64)>{let idx=[(va>>39)&511,(va>>30)&511,(va>>21)&511,(va>>12)&511];let mut table=self.pml4;let mut effective=u64::MAX;for &i in &idx[..3]{let e=unsafe{a.table(table)}[i as usize];if e&PRESENT==0||e&(1<<7)!=0{return None}effective&=e;table=e&ADDR;}let e=unsafe{a.table(table)}[idx[3]as usize];if e&PRESENT==0{None}else{Some(((e&ADDR)|(va&4095),effective&e))}}

 pub fn clone_bootstrap<A:PhysAccess>(&self,a:&A,private_slots:&[usize])->Result<Self,i64>{let out=Self::new(a)?;let src=unsafe{a.table(self.pml4)};let dst=unsafe{a.table(out.pml4)};dst.copy_from_slice(src);for &slot in private_slots{if slot>=512{return Err(crate::errno::EINVAL)}dst[slot]=0;}Ok(out)}
 pub fn new<A:PhysAccess>(a:&A)->Result<Self,i64>{let pa=memory::alloc_frame().ok_or(crate::errno::ENOMEM)?;unsafe{a.table(pa).fill(0)}Ok(Self{pml4:pa})}
 pub fn map_page<A:PhysAccess>(&self,a:&A,va:u64,pa:u64,flags:u64)->Result<(),i64>{if va&4095!=0||pa&4095!=0{return Err(crate::errno::EINVAL)}let idx=[(va>>39)&511,(va>>30)&511,(va>>21)&511,(va>>12)&511];let mut table=self.pml4;for &i in &idx[..3]{let t=unsafe{a.table(table)};let e=&mut t[i as usize];if *e&PRESENT==0{let n=memory::alloc_frame().ok_or(crate::errno::ENOMEM)?;unsafe{a.table(n).fill(0)};*e=n|PRESENT|WRITABLE|if flags&USER!=0{USER}else{0};}else if *e&(1<<7)!=0{return Err(crate::errno::EINVAL)}if flags&USER!=0{*e|=USER}table=*e&ADDR;}let leaf=unsafe{a.table(table)};if leaf[idx[3]as usize]&PRESENT!=0{return Err(crate::errno::EEXIST)}leaf[idx[3]as usize]=(pa&ADDR)|PRESENT|flags;unsafe{invlpg(va)}Ok(())}
 pub fn unmap_page<A:PhysAccess>(&self,a:&A,va:u64)->Result<u64,i64>{let e=self.walk_mut(a,va)?;if *e&PRESENT==0{return Err(crate::errno::ENOENT)}let pa=*e&ADDR;*e=0;unsafe{invlpg(va)}Ok(pa)}
 pub fn protect_page<A:PhysAccess>(&self,a:&A,va:u64,flags:u64)->Result<(),i64>{let e=self.walk_mut(a,va)?;if *e&PRESENT==0{return Err(crate::errno::ENOENT)}*e=(*e&ADDR)|PRESENT|flags;unsafe{invlpg(va)}Ok(())}
 fn walk_mut<'a,A:PhysAccess>(&self,a:&'a A,va:u64)->Result<&'a mut u64,i64>{let idx=[(va>>39)&511,(va>>30)&511,(va>>21)&511,(va>>12)&511];let mut table=self.pml4;for &i in &idx[..3]{let e=unsafe{a.table(table)}[i as usize];if e&PRESENT==0{return Err(crate::errno::ENOENT)}table=e&ADDR;}Ok(&mut unsafe{a.table(table)}[idx[3]as usize])}
 pub fn destroy_private_slot<A:PhysAccess>(&self,a:&A,slot:usize)->Result<(),i64>{if slot>=256{return Err(crate::errno::EINVAL)}
 let pml4=unsafe{a.table(self.pml4)};let top=pml4[slot];
 if top&PRESENT==0{pml4[slot]=0;return Ok(())}
 if top&(1<<7)!=0{return Err(crate::errno::EINVAL)}let pdpt_pa=top&ADDR;let pdpt=unsafe{a.table(pdpt_pa)};for i in 0..512{let e3=pdpt[i];if e3&PRESENT==0{continue}if e3&(1<<7)!=0{return Err(crate::errno::EINVAL)}let pd_pa=e3&ADDR;let pd=unsafe{a.table(pd_pa)};for j in 0..512{let e2=pd[j];if e2&PRESENT==0{continue}if e2&(1<<7)!=0{return Err(crate::errno::EINVAL)}let pt_pa=e2&ADDR;let pt=unsafe{a.table(pt_pa)};for k in 0..512{let e1=pt[k];if e1&PRESENT!=0{crate::memory::release_frame(e1&ADDR)?;pt[k]=0;}}if let Err(e)=crate::memory::free_frame(pt_pa){return Err(e)}pd[j]=0;}
    if let Err(e)=crate::memory::free_frame(pd_pa){return Err(e)}pdpt[i]=0;}
   if let Err(e)=crate::memory::free_frame(pdpt_pa){return Err(e)}pml4[slot]=0;Ok(())}
 pub unsafe fn activate(&self){unsafe{core::arch::asm!("mov cr3, {}",in(reg)self.pml4,options(nostack,preserves_flags));}}
 /* Installs the kernel direct map: `slots` PML4 entries of 512 GiB each at
    PHYS_BASE, backed by 1 GiB pages covering the whole physical address
    space (RAM and MMIO alike). Runs before the kernel moves to the high
    half, so it must be given an identity accessor. */
 pub fn install_direct_map<A:PhysAccess>(&self,a:&A,slots:usize)->Result<(),&'static str>{
  let base_slot=((crate::arch::x86_64::phys::PHYS_BASE>>39)&511)as usize;
  for s in 0..slots{
   if base_slot+s>=512{return Err("direct map exceeds PML4")}
   let pdpt=memory::alloc_frame().ok_or("direct map allocation failed")?;
   let t=unsafe{a.table(pdpt)};
   for i in 0..512{t[i]=(((s*512+i)as u64)<<30)|PRESENT|WRITABLE|(1<<7);}
   let p=unsafe{a.table(self.pml4)};
   p[base_slot+s]=pdpt|PRESENT|WRITABLE;
  }
  Ok(())
 }
 /* Zeroes every PML4 entry except `keep`: used once after the move to the
    high half to drop all firmware mappings in one step. */
 pub unsafe fn keep_slots<A:PhysAccess>(&self,a:&A,keep:&[usize]){
  let t=unsafe{a.table(self.pml4)};
  for i in 0..512{if !keep.contains(&i){t[i]=0}}
  unsafe{core::arch::asm!("mov cr3, {}",in(reg)self.pml4,options(nostack,preserves_flags));}
 }
}
#[inline]pub unsafe fn invlpg(va:u64){unsafe{core::arch::asm!("invlpg [{}]",in(reg)va,options(nostack,preserves_flags));}}
/* One-way move to the high half. `rsp` and `entry` are direct-map addresses;
   the firmware page table stays active but already contains the direct map. */
#[unsafe(naked)]pub unsafe extern "sysv64" fn enter_high(rsp:u64,entry:u64)->!{core::arch::naked_asm!("mov rsp, rdi","jmp rsi")}

//! Physical access through the kernel's high-half direct map.
//!
//! After `kmain_high_continue` the kernel runs in the high half and owns no
//! low mappings, so every physical address is reached through the direct map
//! installed by `paging::install_direct_map` at `PHYS_BASE`.
use super::paging::PhysAccess;
/// Base of the direct physical map: PML4 slot 256 (canonical high half).
pub const PHYS_BASE:u64=0xffff_8000_0000_0000;
/// Where the kernel relocates its image at boot: PML4 slot 288, outside the
/// direct map so 4 KiB pages can back it.
pub const KIMAGE_BASE:u64=0xffff_9000_0000_0000;
#[inline] pub const fn phys_to_virt(pa:u64)->u64{PHYS_BASE+pa}
/// Translates a kernel virtual address through the active page tables. Covers
/// both the direct map and the relocated kernel image; returns 0 if unmapped.
pub fn virt_to_phys(va:u64)->u64{
    let root=super::paging::PageTableRoot::current();
    root.translate(&Identity,va).map(|e|(e&super::paging::ADDR)|(va&0xfff)).unwrap_or(0)
}
pub struct Identity;
impl PhysAccess for Identity {
    unsafe fn table(&self, pa:u64)->&mut[u64;512] {
        unsafe { &mut *(phys_to_virt(pa) as *mut [u64;512]) }
    }
}
impl Identity {
    pub unsafe fn bytes(&self,pa:u64,len:usize)->&mut[u8]{unsafe{core::slice::from_raw_parts_mut(phys_to_virt(pa) as *mut u8,len)}}
}
/// Identity accessor used only while building the direct map, before the
/// kernel can address the high half.
pub struct EarlyPhys;
impl PhysAccess for EarlyPhys {
    unsafe fn table(&self, pa:u64)->&mut[u64;512] {
        unsafe { &mut *(pa as *mut [u64;512]) }
    }
}
impl EarlyPhys {
    pub unsafe fn bytes(&self,pa:u64,len:usize)->&mut[u8]{unsafe{core::slice::from_raw_parts_mut(pa as *mut u8,len)}}
}

//! QEMU isa-debug-exit support for deterministic acceptance tests.
const PORT:u16=0xf4;
/// With isa-debug-exit, QEMU returns host status `(value << 1) | 1`.
pub fn exit(value:u8)->!{
    crate::console::flush();
    unsafe{core::arch::asm!("out dx, eax",in("dx")PORT,in("eax")value as u32,options(nomem,nostack,preserves_flags));}
    crate::arch::halt()
}

//! CMOS/RTC wall-clock read used to seed CLOCK_REALTIME at boot.
use super::port::{inb,outb};

fn read(reg:u8)->u8{unsafe{outb(0x70,reg);inb(0x71)}}

fn bcd(v:u8)->u64{((v>>4)as u64)*10+(v&0xf)as u64}

fn status_ok()->bool{unsafe{outb(0x70,0x0D);inb(0x71)&0x80!=0}}

/* Reads the RTC in binary or BCD mode; returns UTC seconds since the epoch. */
pub fn epoch_seconds()->Option<u64>{
 for _ in 0..1000{unsafe{outb(0x70,0x0A);if inb(0x71)&0x80==0{break}}}
 let st=read(0x0B);
 let bcd_mode=st&0x04==0;
 let h12=st&0x02!=0;
 let conv=if bcd_mode{bcd}else{|v|v as u64};
 let mut sec=conv(read(0x00));
 let min=conv(read(0x02));
 let hraw=read(0x04);
 let mut hour=conv(hraw&0x7f);
 let day=conv(read(0x07));
 let mon=conv(read(0x08));
 let year=conv(read(0x09));
 let mut century=conv(read(0x32)&0x7f);
 if h12{
  let pm=hraw&0x80!=0;
  if pm&&hour<12{hour+=12}
  if !pm&&hour==12{hour=0}
 }
 if century==0{century=if year>=70{19}else{20}}
 if sec>=60||min>=60||hour>=24||day<1||day>31||mon<1||mon>12||year>199||century<19||century>21{return None}
 if !status_ok(){return None}
 sec=sec+min*60+hour*3600;
 let mut days=0u64;
 let y=century*100+year;
 for yy in 1970..y{
  days+=if leap(yy){366}else{365};
 }
 let mdays=[31u64,28,31,30,31,30,31,31,30,31,30,31];
 for mm in 0..(mon as usize-1){
  days+=mdays[mm];
  if mm==1&&leap(y){days+=1}
 }
 days+=day-1;
 Some(days*86400+sec)
}

fn leap(y:u64)->bool{y%4==0&&(y%100!=0||y%400==0)}

use crate::linux_abi;
use super::{descriptors,msr};

unsafe extern "C" { fn syscall_entry(); }
#[unsafe(no_mangle)] pub static mut SYSCALL_KERNEL_RSP:u64=0;
#[unsafe(no_mangle)] pub static mut SYSCALL_USER_RSP:u64=0;
/* Entry point of the saved user register frame, published for fork. */
#[unsafe(no_mangle)] pub static mut SYSCALL_FRAME:u64=0;
/* Scratch cell for the dispatcher result between the dispatcher call and the
   user-mode return. */
#[unsafe(no_mangle)] pub static mut SYSCALL_RESULT:u64=0;
/* The value rt_sigreturn restores into the user register file. It is
   published separately because the dispatcher writes the scratch cell above
   after the syscall returns. */
#[unsafe(no_mangle)] pub static mut SIGRET_VALUE:u64=0;
#[unsafe(no_mangle)] pub static mut SIGRET_VALID:u8=0;
/* User selectors used by the IRETQ return path of the syscall entry. */
#[unsafe(no_mangle)] pub static USER_CS:u64=descriptors::UCODE as u64;
#[unsafe(no_mangle)] pub static USER_SS:u64=descriptors::UDATA as u64;

/* Register snapshot taken while a syscall is being serviced. Field order and
   offsets are the on-stack frame layout in syscall.S. */
#[derive(Clone,Copy)]
#[repr(C)]
pub struct UserFrame{pub r15:u64,pub r14:u64,pub r13:u64,pub r12:u64,pub rbp:u64,pub rbx:u64,pub r10:u64,pub r9:u64,pub r8:u64,pub rdx:u64,pub rsi:u64,pub rdi:u64,pub rax:u64,pub rip:u64,pub rflags:u64,pub rsp:u64}

pub unsafe fn init(kernel_stack_top:u64){
 unsafe{
  SYSCALL_KERNEL_RSP=kernel_stack_top;
  let mut efer=msr::rdmsr(msr::IA32_EFER);efer|=1|(1<<11);msr::wrmsr(msr::IA32_EFER,efer);
  let star=((descriptors::UCODE as u64-16)<<48)|((descriptors::KCODE as u64)<<32);
  msr::wrmsr(msr::IA32_STAR,star);msr::wrmsr(msr::IA32_LSTAR,syscall_entry as usize as u64);
  msr::wrmsr(msr::IA32_FMASK,(1<<9)|(1<<10));
 }
}
#[unsafe(no_mangle)]
pub extern "sysv64" fn syscall_dispatch_raw(nr:u64,a1:u64,a2:u64,a3:u64,a4:u64,a5:u64,a6:u64)->i64{
 if nr>=200||nr==41||nr==42||nr==43||nr==49||nr==50||nr==57{}
 crate::task::stack_guard();
 let r=linux_abi::dispatch(nr,[a1,a2,a3,a4,a5,a6]);

}

/* Offsets of the on-stack user register frame saved by syscall_entry. */
const F_R15:usize=0;const F_R14:usize=1;const F_R13:usize=2;const F_R12:usize=3;const F_RBP:usize=4;const F_RBX:usize=5;

/* Size and layout of the published Linux rt_sigframe. */
fn canonical_user(v:u64)->bool{v>=crate::usercopy::USER_MIN as u64&&v<crate::usercopy::USER_MAX as u64}
/* Runs before the syscall returns to user mode. Returns 1 when a signal was
   prepared and the user context was rewritten for the handler entry. */
#[unsafe(no_mangle)]
pub extern "sysv64" fn signal_pending_check(frame:*mut u64)->u64{
 if frame.is_null(){return 0}
 match crate::signal::prepare_delivery(frame){
  None=>0,
  Some(d)=>{
   let mut ctx=match capture_user_frame(frame){Some(f)=>f,None=>return 0};
   ctx.rip=d.rip;ctx.rsp=d.rsp;ctx.rflags=d.rflags;
   ctx.rdi=d.sig as u64;ctx.rsi=d.siginfo;ctx.rdx=d.ucontext;
   if set_return_context(frame,&ctx){1}else{0}
  }
 }
}
/* Copies the in-flight syscall frame into a resume snapshot whose RAX is 0,
    the value fork must return in the child. Callers run in syscall context. */
pub fn capture_fork_frame()->Option<UserFrame>{
 let f=unsafe{SYSCALL_FRAME as *const u64};if f.is_null(){return None}
 unsafe{
  Some(UserFrame{
   r15:*f.add(0),r14:*f.add(1),r13:*f.add(2),r12:*f.add(3),rbp:*f.add(4),rbx:*f.add(5),
   r10:*f.add(6),r9:*f.add(7),r8:*f.add(8),rdx:*f.add(9),rsi:*f.add(10),rdi:*f.add(11),
   rax:0,rip:*f.add(13),rflags:*f.add(14),rsp:SYSCALL_USER_RSP,
  })
 }
}

/* rt_sigreturn: restores the interrupted context from the handler frame the
   kernel wrote before entering the handler. */
pub unsafe fn signal_restore_frame()->i64{
 match crate::signal::restore_frame(){Ok(())=>0,Err(e)=>crate::errno::neg(e)}
}

/* Copies the in-flight syscall frame into a snapshot of the interrupted user
   context. Callers run in syscall context. */
pub fn capture_user_frame(frame:*mut u64)->Option<UserFrame>{
 let f=frame as *const u64;if f.is_null(){return None}
 unsafe{
  Some(UserFrame{
   r15:*f.add(0),r14:*f.add(1),r13:*f.add(2),r12:*f.add(3),rbp:*f.add(4),rbx:*f.add(5),
   r10:*f.add(6),r9:*f.add(7),r8:*f.add(8),rdx:*f.add(9),rsi:*f.add(10),rdi:*f.add(11),
   rax:*f.add(12),rip:*f.add(13),rflags:*f.add(14),rsp:SYSCALL_USER_RSP,
  })
 }
}

/* Rewrites the user context that the syscall return path restores. */
pub fn set_return_context(frame:*mut u64,ctx:&UserFrame)->bool{
 let f=frame;if f.is_null(){return false}
 unsafe{
  *f.add(0)=ctx.r15;*f.add(1)=ctx.r14;*f.add(2)=ctx.r13;*f.add(3)=ctx.r12;
  *f.add(4)=ctx.rbp;*f.add(5)=ctx.rbx;*f.add(6)=ctx.r10;*f.add(7)=ctx.r9;
  *f.add(8)=ctx.r8;*f.add(9)=ctx.rdx;*f.add(10)=ctx.rsi;*f.add(11)=ctx.rdi;
  *f.add(12)=ctx.rax;*f.add(13)=ctx.rip;*f.add(14)=ctx.rflags;
  SYSCALL_USER_RSP=ctx.rsp;
 }
 true
}

//! Stable hand-off ABI between a firmware loader and the kernel.
pub const BOOT_MAGIC: u64 = 0x534c4f50_4d4e5521; // "SLOPMNU!"
pub const BOOT_ABI_VERSION: u32 = 2;

#[repr(C)]
#[derive(Clone, Copy)]
pub struct MemoryRegion { pub base: u64, pub pages: u64, pub kind: u32, pub attributes: u32 }

#[repr(C)]
#[derive(Clone, Copy)]
pub struct Framebuffer { pub base: u64, pub size: u64, pub width: u32, pub height: u32, pub stride: u32, pub format: u32 }

#[repr(C)]
pub struct BootInfo {
    pub magic: u64, pub abi_version: u32, pub size: u32,
    pub memory_map: *const MemoryRegion, pub memory_map_len: usize,
    pub rsdp: u64, pub initrd_base: u64, pub initrd_size: u64,
    pub framebuffer: Framebuffer,
    pub image_base: u64, pub image_size: u64,
}
impl BootInfo {
    pub fn validate(&self) -> bool {
        self.magic == BOOT_MAGIC && self.abi_version == BOOT_ABI_VERSION &&
        self.size as usize >= core::mem::size_of::<BootInfo>() &&
        (self.memory_map_len == 0 || !self.memory_map.is_null())
    }
}

/* Applies PE32+ base relocations (`delta` = new base - original base) to a
   loaded copy of the kernel image so it can execute at its new base. Only
   DIR64 entries exist in images built by the LLVM toolchain. */
pub unsafe fn apply_relocations(image:u64,delta:i64)->Result<usize,&'static str>{
    let base=image as *const u8;
    let rd16=|off:u64|unsafe{core::ptr::read_unaligned(base.add(off as usize)as*const u16)};
    let rd32=|off:u64|unsafe{core::ptr::read_unaligned(base.add(off as usize)as*const u32)};
    if rd16(0)!=0x5a4d{return Err("image is not a PE")}
    let pe=rd32(0x3c)as u64;
    if rd32(pe)!=0x0000_4550{return Err("bad PE signature")}
    let opt=pe+24;
    if rd16(opt)!=0x20b{return Err("image is not PE32+")}
    let dirs=rd32(opt+108)as u64;
    if dirs<6{return Err("no base relocation directory")}
    let reloc_rva=rd32(opt+112+5*8)as u64;
    let reloc_size=rd32(opt+112+5*8+4)as u64;
    if reloc_rva==0||reloc_size<8{return Err("base relocations stripped")}
    let mut p=reloc_rva;
    let end=reloc_rva+reloc_size;
    let mut applied=0usize;
    while p+8<=end{
        let page=rd32(p)as u64;
        let block=rd32(p+4)as u64;
        if block<8||p+block>end{return Err("corrupt relocation block")}
        let mut q=p+8;
        while q+2<=p+block{
            let e=rd16(q);
            let typ=e>>12;
            let off=(e&0xfff)as u64;
            if typ==10{
                let at=base.add((page+off)as usize)as*mut u64;
                unsafe{let v=core::ptr::read_unaligned(at);core::ptr::write_unaligned(at,(v as i64+delta)as u64)};
                applied+=1;
            }else if typ!=0{return Err("unsupported relocation type")}
            q+=2;
        }
        p+=block;
    }
    Ok(applied)
}

use core::fmt::{self,Write};
const COM1:u16=0x3f8;const LSR:u16=COM1+5;const THR_EMPTY:u8=1<<5;const TX_EMPTY:u8=1<<6;
struct Serial;
impl Write for Serial{fn write_str(&mut self,s:&str)->fmt::Result{for b in s.bytes(){write_byte(b)}Ok(())}}
#[inline]unsafe fn outb(port:u16,value:u8){unsafe{core::arch::asm!("out dx, al",in("dx")port,in("al")value,options(nomem,nostack,preserves_flags));}}
#[inline]unsafe fn inb(port:u16)->u8{let value:u8;unsafe{core::arch::asm!("in al, dx",out("al")value,in("dx")port,options(nomem,nostack,preserves_flags));}value}
fn wait(mask:u8){for _ in 0..1_000_000{if unsafe{inb(LSR)}&mask==mask{return}}}
fn write_byte(byte:u8){if byte==b'\n'{wait(THR_EMPTY);unsafe{outb(COM1,b'\r')}}wait(THR_EMPTY);unsafe{outb(COM1,byte)}}
pub fn init(){unsafe{outb(COM1+1,0);outb(COM1+3,0x80);outb(COM1,3);outb(COM1+1,0);outb(COM1+3,3);outb(COM1+2,0xc7);outb(COM1+4,0x0b);}}
pub fn flush(){wait(TX_EMPTY)}
pub fn write_bytes(bytes:&[u8]){for &b in bytes{write_byte(b)}}
pub fn print(args:fmt::Arguments){let _=Serial.write_fmt(args);}
#[macro_export]macro_rules! print{($($arg:tt)*)=>($crate::console::print(format_args!($($arg)*)));}
#[macro_export]macro_rules! println{()=>($crate::print!("\n"));($fmt:expr)=>($crate::print!(concat!($fmt,"\n")));($fmt:expr,$($arg:tt)*)=>($crate::print!(concat!($fmt,"\n"),$($arg)*));}
